I just don't like the way the hotkeys work in Ned. The 'r' and 'e' keys are too close together, and when I'm playtesting a map, I'll die (often) and hit 'r', but occasionally, I'll accidentally hit 'e'. This:
a- screws up the position of N
b- screws up the position of any thwump, zap drone, laser drone, chaingun drone, or floor guard.
And in DDA's, when I have lots of moving walls, and I sometimes position N at the start with 'z' snap placement. This is sooooooo hard to re-do, if you accidentally push 'e' instead of 'r'.
If other people are angry about this, maybe we can get it changed...? Maybe!

Original Contest Description[/u]

This is a competition based around the well known phrase, "Less is More".
In my opinion, this is quite true, and this challenge will test your skills in creating great maps that are very *very* simple.

Here are the rules:

1) No more than 5 tiles
2) No more than 5 objects
3) No more than 3 entries per person.
4) Demo must be posted by author of under 500 frames

N counts as an object
A door (and its respective switch) counts as one object

Judging:

A very basic judging system: Out of 50, we will rate the map, taking into consideration how fun and exciting it is.

Judges for this competition:


Jiggerjaw12358
Sweep
Wellsj

Back-up Judge:
Guiseppi

#115850 41 2005-11-18T17:48:10Z Jiggerjaw
I don't know if this has been suggested yet, as I do not want to sit around all day reading a thread, but, what if we use this system:

You base it on # of people who favorited the map, not on the avg. rating. This way, there is no possible way to ruin a person's map. By favoriting a map, it goes up in the standings, by not favoriting the map, it stays where it is.

This way, no awesome maps get sniped, they just don't get that extra boost from a n00b who doesn't like the person. And n00b maps will certainly not be favorited, or maybe just once by another n00b, this way those maps are terrible.

Also, perhaps there could be a back-up rating system-a-ma-bobber to determine a map's initial quality, so that people know which maps to look at (as the rating system is not ENTIRELY faulty).

No spite votes, no n00bish maps ending up on top, it's a considerably good idea IMO. In fact, I love my idea now more than I did when I thought it up (though I know it's not an original idea, don't get angry at me for that).

Just a suggestion, that's all.

Pinko's "Pimpo"

It was an interesting map...unfortunately with the limitations of this competition, you could simply get the switch, go to the bottom, and then jump back up to the door. However, when done in the correct manner, this map is quite fun. Well done, Pimpo, but it's not wonderful or anything.

28/50

Virtual_Cyberspace's "Less is More"

Less is indeed more. This was quite hard, I in fact couldn't beat it myself, I am a very bad player (who did beat N ) I very much enjoyed this, very challenging, and very fun. I liked that you had that little jumper puzzle-esque part with the mine at the beginning, whee!

37/50

I_Spy's "Incompident"

I really don't like a cheatable map (don't complain about the limitations, you knew what you were getting into with this competition). This map was not really that fun, and certainly not challenging (especially with the cheat). Luckily for you, I'm in a good mood...

27/50

Wellsj's "Barracks"

Wow, another awesome submission from wells...you guys have alot of competition with him, one more week to submit maps and avoid him mopping the floor with your face (see that his other 2 maps are tied for best 2-rating map so far).

Anyway, on the map, this is a fun, simplistic action-type map with the thrilling chaingun drone adding a tinge of difficulty. I really love that tinge, that's what makes a submission to this competition great, and I stress that, people...Really good job, wells.

39/50

Krusch's "Interpol"

A neat little drone map with 5 '8' tiles. Quite fun, the first real "Jumper-puzzle" submitted. However, it *is* cheatable, and that'll take your rating down, I'm afraid. It was very fun, don't get me wrong, but the true challenge of this competition is to make uncheatable maps. Still, quite a fun map.

32/50

Atramentis' "Strive or Dive"

Unfortunately, Atramentis has left us, as have his wonderful maps, this one among them...we'll miss you Atra...

Krusch's "Estapol"

Holy mother of god! Wow! Wonderful map, the best so far in my opinion. This is why. You made incredible use of the "Jagged Jeopardy" idea by tktktk for such a limited competition. This map is very *very* hard without fbf. Wow...you truly earned this:

47/50

Klumpeet's "Jumps"

This is a pretty weak entry...it's very easy (requires only 1 jump) and I get the impression that you (and many others) just want to use the full 5 objects, so you used those one-ways as filler, seriously pointless. I'm sorry, but this entry is just weak.

21/50

MasterJmd's "Inner"

This map was a puzzle (not too puzzling, but a puzzle) that uses the '1' tile double jump Jumper-puzzle technique, as well as the "Tight Squeeze" innovation. It is very easy, but somehow fun in it's own little way. I think the enjoyment of this map comes with trying to get the fastest time. A pretty fun entry, but it's still a bit mediocre I'm afraid.

34/50

Spzbt/Spondoogie's "I'm too annoying to use regular characters"

It's easy, and not too fun, but I must admit, I was taken by surprise when I fell through the one-way, AAH! Um, a suggestion about that, why not suspend it higher so that when you do fall through, you die, not just stand there, with the ability to just walljump back up and start over again.

24/50

Pariah/Astheoceansblue's "Something Vague 2"

I felt this map to be very lacking in entertainment, enjoyability, difficulty, etc. It was just lacking in all aspects. It's just not a great submission, I'm sorry to say...

22/50

Jtrox's "Why do I HAYTE Triangles?"

This was quite frankly a stupid map. A ) The thwumps added no challenge whatsoever, you could've used your allotted 3 objects to a so much further extent. B ) I thought this was a jumper-puzzle at first...I was sadly mistaken.

23/50

I was just wondering this one day. There are some really weird usernames out there on the forums, and on Numa, and I'd like to know where your usernames originated. How did it come about?

My username:
A pretty boring story, really...Me and my friend (Guiseppi on the forums, if you were wondering) were playing chess during lunch at our school. Everytime I made a really good move, I would loudly whisper "Jiggerjaw" really fast. It just became my trademark saying, along with...other things
The 12358 is a mathematical code called Fibonacci sequence, in which you add the previous number to the number you're on to get the next number. It's actually 112358, but when I made my email address with that number, a long time ago, I wasn't thinking clearly.

What is the origin of *your* name?

#5480 1 2005-12-01T12:51:01Z Jiggerjaw
If you take a look at my map, "Mountains come out of the Sky..." and the title goes on, you'll see that I tried to incorporate "beating the laser drone". Basically, you have to move faster than the laser in order to survive. To my dismay, however, all you have to do is go even slower in order to avoid death. In order for it to have worked, there would have to be some way for the laser drone to make it so that you could not win. Like a trap or locked door only pressable by a drone. This could open all kinds of new possibilities in the world of traps. At the end of "Mountains come out...", you notice that if you go fast enough, the laser will end up trapped, but if you go too slow, the drone will end up in the narrow shaft on the left. This would be good without the magic of really fast wall jumping, as the drone would then be impassible, but you can just jump past the thing before it fired. However, if there were a drone-only trap door switch in that shaft that blocked you off from finishing the level, the intensity of the "beat the laser drone" technique is heightened considerably. I really like this idea, however, I just don't know how hard it would be for Mare & Raigan to add this in to the programming for Nv1.5. Please tell me what you think of the idea, and tell me if you think it would be easy or hard to incorporate it into the game.

Thanks.

#119796 5 2005-12-02T12:57:11Z Jiggerjaw
No, no, no, I think what he means is how to get the level into userlvls...What you do:

You can't just paste that level data into userlevels.txt, you have to do it in this format:

$Name of map#Author#Category#Map data#

Then you can play it from your user levels menu!

My final judging list:

Kamikaze3000's "High Flying"

There is just clearly no effort put into this map. It does not require any skill whatsoever. You fall, the chaingun misses you, you jump over it, you don't get yourself dead by the mine, and you win. Way to easy, and overall not fun.

23/50

Blazeraid's "Nice and Simple"

It is not a very fun level, but most certainly an innovative one. I don't think I've ever seen this before. It requires a lot of precision, and I actually enjoyed it a bit. Maybe create something like this idea with tiles, because this is still too simple.

31/50

Deadly's "Colony"

This is a score! Very fun and entertaining. If only you were alotted more objects, you could prevent a wall jump start, forcing the player to make a difficult first jump. I mean who's stupid enough to come up with a rule like "only 5 objects"? What a dumbass! Wow, went off on a tangent, I have TERRIBLE ADD!! Back to the map, I really liked it, well done

38/50

AlliedEnvy's "Point Blank"

Wow, this is a really hard map! You gotta go under that tile, but not too far, or *whistle* *splat*! It really takes precision. I couldn't beat it until I watched a demo. Very, very good map Allied, I enjoyed it thoroughly.

40/50

Klumpeet's "Go for the Gold?"

The challenge of this map is not completion, though that is rather hard, the challenge comes with getting those gold pieces. That was really hard. Falling down was quite frustrating for me, and it happened so often. It didn't really seem like you used the alloted tiles to there full potential. I do like this, but it's not the best, by any stretch.

34/50

Sketch's "HotShot"

Perhaps you didn't intend it, Sketch, but this *could* have been a really good KRA map. It wasn't necessary to K the R A, but a fun little added challenge. I must say, this competition has come a *long* way since it's beginning, and in the end, it'll be a very close call at the end to decide the winner. This wasn't a great map, but it kept me entertained for 3 or 4 tries (considerably good, once again, ADD)

27/50

MasterJmd's "Bunker"

Using the thwump accelerated tight squeeze method made for neat gameplay. It's not by any stretch of the imagination original (see so many other entries), but it utilizes fun little techniques in an interesting and fun way. I enjoyed it for sure.

36/50

Villemancer's "Noclip"

Wow, dear lord, that's good. I really loved this, I couldn't do it AT ALL! I watched your demo and my jaw fell to the floor. Jeeze, really good! Really, really good.

45/50

Fuzzle's "N's Favorite Meal"

Ick. I really didn't like this. Going for the gold was not un-fun, but that chaingun drone didn't do crap! It didn't threaten you at all. You only used 3 objects, kinda a no-no in my book, and it's just not a fun map. Sorry.

19/50

Krusch's "Sebastapol"

This was the worst of Krusch's three entries. That's not to say its terrible, but it's cheatable, a no-no, and not very challenging, another no-no. As long as you don't run right at it, the rocket is not a big challenge. The fun of the map is going for speedy gold demos, but it's just not a great entry.

25/50

Ngaged's "Square Hop (redux)"

So, so, so, sooooo much better. Completely uncheatable, great! This is a really toughy, I don't have trouble with corner kicks, but a double corner kick is really hard, I really liked this map. Fun and hard, in perfect harmony for a good entry to the Simplicity Competition.

37/50

Sketch's "The Parasite"

*drool* Holy mother of God, this is absolutely incredible. Try as I might, I cannot beat this map. To tell you how much I admire it, if I were to see this on Numa, I would give it a 4. And that's not how I vote (it's how Sweep and wells vote but I vote a tad higher than that). This is the best map submitted, Krusch's "Estapol" is a very close second, but rating comparitively, this is what you get. What you deserve:

48/50

TDL/formica's "Simplicitally"

Wow, that gold piece is tough as hell to get. It's hard not to be biased as TDL is my 2nd favorite author (#1 is wells, if you're wondering), but I'll try. This was a good map under the restrictions given. It's not too hard unless you're going for the gold, but it's very fun. It has a good balance of difficulty and entertainment. If I would've remade this map, I'd take out the gold and put a rocket, guass, or chaingun somewhere below, making for a really nice challenge. This is not bad, though.

31/50

Sucker's "Falling and Squishing"

Wow, this really requires some speed. A great improvement on your first one, however, if you recall, that was already a 42...and as I rate comparitively, and don't feel this is as good as Estapol, I can't give it above a 46. It's a really fun map, and I think it deserves this:

45/50

Rrrobyp's "QTRT"

Ooh, a drone path racing map. Neat, neat, neat. That drone gave me serious trouble when I tried it out, which makes this map a good entry. Very enjoyable, but not a really awesome or ambitious map. But you definitely get points for originality, as this is the first map anything like this to be submitted. It's average, though, not bad, not amazing.

28/50

Guiseppi's "Pump up the Volume...OF CHEESE!"

What a dumb name lol! Cheatable=yuk! It's a shame, because without that cheat, it's a real tough jumper. Oh well, you made an attempt, BreJus...

24/50

Sketch's "Inner Evil"

You have to jump after 5 frames, which is a really tough feat without fbf. This is officially the only KRA map submitted to the Simplicity Competition, surprisingly. Of course, under the criteria set forth by my competition, it is very easy to K the R A, but it's the thought that counts.

29/50

Wow. 62 entries judged by me. I remember when I submitted Jiggerjaw's SC 2, 3, and 4, and thought, hmm, I'm just using 3-4 tiles and no objects...then I asked my buddy, Guiseppi, "What if I started a competition based on my new Jiggerjaw's SC maps? Like maybe only 5 objects and 5 tiles." And he said to me, "No, that's really stupid!" And I replied, "Yeah, you're probably right." And look how far it's come. Wow. Come on! Gimme more! Oh...what? #125054 4 2005-12-20T04:37:27Z Jiggerjaw
Format
~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~
$Map Name#Author#Category#Map Data#
~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~
Just put that into the userlevels.txt file, then go into N, into userlevels, and press 'refresh list' then it will parse the data, and your map will be found in the list. Hope this info helped.

#125056 21 2005-12-20T04:45:56Z Jiggerjaw
And if you want to register to Numa, go to the 'configure' menu in N, and type in a username, password, and your email address in the fields in the lower right hand of the screen. Click 'create new user'. MetaNet will then send you an activation e-mail with a link in it. Click the link to activate your account. Then you can sign on on Numa.

#127567 4 2005-12-28T07:28:24Z Jiggerjaw
And to add items to the userlevels.txt file, found in the folder that N is in, use this format:
$Map Name#Author#Category#Map Data#
Just place that in there, then go into userlevels and press 'refresh list'.
Then you're on your way.
Hope we could help, but really, you should read the manual.
I'm not normally this lenient.

#128675 7 2005-12-31T07:25:59Z Jiggerjaw
I have the current (as of yesterday) top 1000 maps in my userlevels.
I don't know if there's a limit, but there is a limit to one's patience when waiting for the damn thing to load. If you have more levels in your userlevels.txt file than in your N game, click File -> Find or Ctrl+F (windows) and search for '&'. Barabajagal's map's Tumble & Tipsy and The New New Testament have always given me trouble. Just find the & and delete all of that junk (like &amp= or something) and refresh list. I hope that works for you.

#129495 3 2006-01-03T18:18:43Z Jiggerjaw
Also, you can copy the level data into the file userlevels.txt in this format:
$Map Name#Author#Category#Map Data#
Then go into N, press userlevels, and press refresh list to play the map you've made.
#129496 3 2006-01-03T18:23:06Z Jiggerjaw
Well, I'll try to help you out. Go to http://numa.notdot.net and find the level you want to play. Now, copy the level data from the bottom text box, and paste it into your userlevels.txt file with all of the other level data. Go into N and push userlevels. Then push refresh list, and it'll appear in the list.
Or...
Copy the level data from the top text box from numa and go into N, and then press ~ or \ This'll take you to debug mode, or Ned. Paste the data into the top text box in Ned, click outside the box, and push 'L', then 'P' to play the level. Be sure that CapsLock is on before starting it, or it'll play in frame-by-frame mode.

#129691 6 2006-01-04T15:41:20Z Jiggerjaw
A DDA with no gold delay shows much more skill and poise in the DDA's author. This is because it's easy to make a DDA with gold delay, because you can make it as long as you want, going over sections time and time again. The omission of gold delay in a DDA takes more skill because the ninja can never go over the same spot twice if it has an object in it (kinda hard to understand that, but you get the picture).
That's my opinion.

Original Competition Information[/u]

The rules[/u]

This is a competition in which you create N art out of only tiles. As a map cannot be submitted to Numa without at least 1 object, you must add one object to your map, and I'd like it to be either hidden by the tiles that make up the level border, or far outside the border, such that it cannot be seen (preferably the former).
The maximum number of submissions by one user is 6.
That's pretty much it, although there is one other rule. If a map is deleted from Numa, it is automatically stricken from the competition. I will be checking Numa periodically to make sure this rule is conformed to.
If a submission is created using N-art Maker, like the original "Brickman's Avatar" by Sucker, it will not be accepted as a viable entry for the competition. I do this because, if it's not your work, then why should I accept it? It's like plagiarism.

The Team[/u]

Jiggerjaw - Judge
Guiseppi - Judge
Monkey of Doom - Judge
Sketch - Back-up Judge
Ultima - Math Checker

The Judging[/u]

Each judge will rate each tileset out of 100 points.
Thumbnail of tileset: Out of 50 points
Full image of tileset: Out of 50 points
The sum of all 3 judges' total scores for each map will be tabulated to determine the final standings.

The Deadline[/u]

The deadline for the competition will be Sunday, February 19, at 12:00 AM GMT sharp. This will (most likely) not be extended for any reason. But if the competition is really nice and vibrant and I decide it needs a bit more time, I may. Also, if anyone comes up with a plausible reason for me to extend it, I'll consider it.

47's "Without the Realm of Possibility"
Thumb: 43
Fullimg: 39
Total: 82
I thought it was a pretty cool tileset, but the only complaint from me is that it was a bit too cluttered. I'd prefer a more simple tileset, but it was really nice. I didn't like the fullimg nearly as much, though.

MasterJmd's "Firestorm Victoriana"
Thumb: 46
Fullimg: 43
Total=89
A truly amazing tileset with lots of potential. The two things that will do well in this competition most definately are artsiness and potential. This is the latter, and fills its description very well. But the most amazing thing is that even with its potential, it's also artsy. That's why it deserves the high mark I gave it.

Sketch's "Telechinisis"
Thumb: 47
Fullimg: 45
Fullimg: 92
Yeah, this is the former, artsy. And amazingly so, as well. Probably the best tileset I've ever seen. Sorry, other submissions. This one'll be tough to beat, guys. Really great job Sketch.

Wellsj's "Shark Attack"
Thumb: 31
Fullimg: 30
Total: 61
Eh. It's artsy, and it probably took quite some effort. But it just didn't do it for me. Sorry. It was still above average, but not entirely amazing.

Sketch's "Klash"
Thumb: 23
Fullimg: 26
Total: 49

Thumb: 37
Fullimg: 39
Total: 78
This is a really amazing usable, flexible tileset. I'm actually considering making a map out of it myself. It would make a really awesome action map, and that's why it deserves a great rating. 4/5 on Numa (don't know why I mentioned that). In a nutshell, very good tileset.

El umi's "Sometimes you just Wonder...Why?"
Thumb: 42
Fullimg: 46
Total: 88

MasterJmd's "Tileset V"
Thumb: 44
Fullimg: 43
Total: 87
Kinda reminds me of fire. That's a great effect in an artsy, usable map like this. I see alot of potential in this tileset, with one exception: The corners. The corners piss me off a bit, because, while continuing the theme of '4' and '8' tiles, I feel that more straightened corners would be better, especially if made into a map, which I expect of this tileset, as it's (like your last submission) quite flexible for many action styles. Good job.

Sarge's "Phobos Anomaly"

#141326 5 2006-02-09T18:10:23Z Jiggerjaw
Well, first of all, you need to be sure that you've actually downloaded MetaNet's real version of N, and are not playing a rip-off version online called "N-game". When you upload the game, in the main menu, press the key ~ or |, and it'll take you to Ned, or debug mode. Now, copy and paste from the text box in a Numa map into the top text box in Ned. Then click outside the text box and press 'L'. Then press 'P' to play the level. Then turn on caps lock to play without frame by frame mode.

#144366 7 2006-02-16T03:11:05Z Jiggerjaw
Wow, I'm happy for Guiseppi and all, but by no means should he be mentioned among these ledgends...I have used the Map Rating Thread to get some of my maps rated, but yeah, once you become known to the community, you get 'fans' who look at your maps regularly and see if you've made new ones. That's why, when you look at several of the top rated authors, you'll notice that many of there maps have been rated, and those that haven't are predominantly test levels and incompletes. Also, everyone has their own style. For example, Pariah, or astheoceansblue, one of my favorite authors, has some really incredible action maps and you'll notice they all seem to have an ongoing style. Klumpeet, whose maps were all reset and hasn't been around for a while, was a great race maker. Much better at races, however (at least in my opinion) is TDL, or formica. My style seems to go with mine jumpers and DDAs with lots of variety. Find your calling and you'll become one of the chosen *choirs of angels sing*
AHEM
So...yeah.

#147460 2 2006-02-23T10:43:53Z Jiggerjaw
Tip: Push button really fast
No kidding, really. Just push jump thrice in succession as fast as your fingers will let you. I've never heard of going too fast, because a perfect triple jump is:
Jump/frame/jump/frame/jump/jump/jump/jump/
What I mean by that is only .050 seconds separates the first and last jump.
You'll get it eventually, and you'll find it's really not that tough. Plus, just to tell you, there was already a topic about this by -naem- in complaints. Do a search next time.

I'll be leaving the forums very soon, and all of you that submitted entries to this competition, thank you very much, but I'm sorry it flopped like hell. Now, I will lock it. If you'd like to keep your submissions, feel free, but I just don't have the time or ambition to keep this competition going. So, I guess you win Sweep. It wasn't the best idea I ever had. Thanks to 9_4_0_0_, although what you did for the competition left a bit to be desired, I thank you for giving me the inspiration to start it...just wish I could finish it. The competition is now over. It won't be judged, and it won't be open to new submissions. If someone wants to carry on in their own thread, feel free, but I have washed my hands of it. It's not open to discussion whether or not I will continue, because I'm a big boy and I can make my own decisions.

My apologies to all those who wanted to see how their entries did...I failed you all, and I'm truly sorry. But, **** happens, as they say, and now it's done.

The only reason I locked this thread as opposed to deleting it is in case someone wants to continue the competition in another thread, they can feel free.

So, peace out, homies!

#9641 1 2006-06-22T18:57:27Z Jiggerjaw
So, here's how this whole thing started...
I was trying desperately to make a simple map. I started with these little nail looking things, staggered in such a way that I was happy with, and I started adding gold. then came the mines, the chaingun, you know the story...but my dad came in at the time of its (almost) completion and said, "What's this supposed to be, railroad spikes?" And so I named it "Driving the Last Spike", after a Genesis (my favorite band) song, and posted some of the lyrics to the song as my description. After doing this, I said, "I should make a series out of this." Out of this came my new series, the Musical Series, an idea which I don't believe has been done before...well, one could argue that Formica's "Tribute" is the same idea, but whatever. On some maps (like Follow You Follow Me), I decided on a song and built the map around that concept, and on other maps (like Once in a Lifetime), I started with an idea, and chose a song to sum up the concept used in the map. Many of these are simple, fun, and fast paced, and I enjoyed the hell out of making them. So, I'll update this post with links to each of the maps as I make them. Use this thread to tell me what you think of the maps! Hope you like playing them as much as I liked making them!

#193122 28 2006-06-22T19:05:00Z Jiggerjaw
The demo is in the upper text box underneath the description of the map. This text box should be labelled "Map Data". Copy and paste that into the upper text box in debug mode (you get there by pressing '~'), click out of the text box (that step is very important), and press 'L'. This loads the map. Then, to record a demo, press '1', and press 'P' to play. Be sure to have caps lock on so you don't have it in frame by frame (necessary only for tough jumps and immense speed). To watch a demo, copy the demo data from the text box (on a map) or from an attached file (on the forums) and paste it into the lower text box in debug mode. Click out of that text box (again - very important), and press '3', followed by 'Q'. Wait for the map to reset (automatically happens by pressing 'Q'), and press 'P', and watch the demo. Then go boil some water and throw in a box of Ramen Noodles and have a good ol' time.

#10924 1 2006-08-28T00:01:53Z Jiggerjaw
The basic idea of this thread is to tell an epic tale of the life of a ninja. Each person that posts in this thread with the intentions of advancing the story must type 1 paragraph minimum. Make it interesting, detailed, and exciting, and above all, be creative. However, don't get crazy and go on about forks and aardvarks for twenty minutes. If need be, your post will be edited for silliness, swearing, suggested themes, you know the deal...

To tickle your tastebuds a bit, let it be known that there is a little extra...secret project in store for this when it's done, so look forward to it.





Quote: The Tale
          One day, long ago, in a galaxy not much different then ours (Except it had floating gold and lazer monsters), A young ninja walked through the forest. He noticed a small grey platform on the ground. Upon stepping on it, he was launched into the air. "I'm flying!" he exclaimed.
          While he was flying in the air, he saw something directly above him. It was only a shadow, but he knew it was something dangerous. As it came into view he could see its color. Red, with spikes on it. 'Now where have I heard about this before...' thought the ninja. Suddenly, he remembered a conversation he had with his grandfather upon leaving his house for the walk in the forest. "Now, when you go out into the forest, there are a couple of things you should watch out for." His grandfather had said. "First and foremost, there are mines. They float in the air. They are red, and have little spikes on them. Even with the slightest touch, they will kill you. It will blow you apart into pieces-" That's all he had to remember to know that he was about to hit a mine, and die! As his entire life flashed before his eyes, he saw a big white square. He remebered what this was right away. "It's a bouceblock!" The little ninja exclaimed. He lunged at it, and found himself sliding down its side wall. He was about to slide off it, and fall from an incredible height! Thinking fast, he jumped off it, gaining a little bit of height. Then, he attached himself right back to it. He repeated that motion 3 more times, until finally, He stood on top of it! It moved down a bit from his impact onto it, but it raised right back up. "I did it!" He exclaimed. Suddenly, it hit him. He was 125 feet off the ground, with practically no way to get back down to the ground. "I don't think my grandpa is going to like this..."
          But then something caught his eye. It was briiight yellow and squared. And he was oddly attracted to it. He thought for a moment what this could be. Then he remembered his grandfather's other story: "One day I had been walking in the woods. I saw something shiny and picked it up. I noticed that I felt much younger. I realised that this shiny thing was the "gold" they talked about in folklore. It could extend your lifespan but it can also hide switches that can trigger traps. So remember, gold will extend your lifespan but it can also be dangerous. So if you see some, DO NOT TOUCH IT!!!" 'Hmm...' the ninja thought. 'There is NO WAY this gold could be a trap' and jumped on it. Suddenly, walls sprang up from all sides! He was trapped. But wait...there were 2 paths out of this trap! But which should he choose?
          One trail led to a field full of mines, while the other lead to some tunnel. Unable to see the other side, and hating mines, N jumped into the tunnel, crawling for two minutes straight. On the other side, though, he found a long stretch that led to some sort of building. He heard multiple cranking sounds, and bullets starting hitting the ground around him.

#278002 33 2006-12-28T17:27:27Z Jiggerjaw
Okay, well here it is, at long last... The brainchild of lord_day, this is a comprehensive DDA Manifesto, which, when asked to touch it up, I took and basically doubled the size of it. We hope you read it all, even though it seems to be a lot, because it contains sh*t-heaps of helpful information on making cool, effective DDAs.

Enjoy...

DDA Manifesto

Written by: lord_day and Jiggerjaw

Index:

What is a DDA?[/u]
List of Different types of DDAs
Propulsion[/u]
Tiles
Launch Pads
Doors
The Different Doors
Trap Door
Normal Door + Trap Door + Locked Door
Normal Door + Gold Delay
Locked Door Glitch
Thwumps
Moving Walls
False Tiles
How to Make False Tiles
What is the Code for That Anyway?
Gold Delay[/u]
Enemies[/u]
The Importance of Enemies
Close Calls
Adding Enemies
Cheating Death
Launch Pad Cheating Death
Exit Door Cheating Death
Enemies that Work
Variety[/u]

~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~

What is a DDA?[/u]

"DDA" stands for Dont Do Anything. A DDA is a map that the player watches as the ninja is moved around by various means. Most DDAs have the ninja finish the level, but some dont, and either purposely kill the ninja off, or make him move around the level endlessly. Some DDAs are more complicated than just a ninja moving around by himself. For example, some maps require the player to hold right or left for the ninja to complete the level. Other variations on the DDA are maps that, for example, keep a rocket alive the entire time, or use only one type of propulsion. Propulsion is what keeps the ninja moving, and without it, very little could happen in a DDA.

List of Different types of DDAs

Dont Do Anything (DDA)
Hold Right Don't Do Anything (HRDDA)Hold Left DDA (HLDDA)
Keep Rocket Alive DDA (KRADDA)
Multiple Way DDA
One Propulsion Method DDA
Demo Does Everything (DDE)

This is by no means the definitive list; all these different types of DDA can be combined, to make all sorts of different DDAs.

Propulsion[/u]

Propulsion is the mechanism that keeps the ninja moving in a DDA so he can travel from A to B. Unlike a normal map, the player does not control the ninja, so the objects in the level have to move the ninja instead. Here is an overview of the most used types of propulsion:

Tiles

It goes without saying that nearly every DDA involves the ninja interacting with tiles. Tiles change the ninjas velocity upon collision. When colliding with tiles the ninja will either change direction or speed, depending on the angle of the tile and the angle of collision. Tiles can also give the ninja a safe place to land if he is falling from a large height. Tiles also add a protection value to the ninja; he is less likely to be hit by enemies if he is protected by tiles. Tiles can also be used to change the ninja's speed. If a ninja is placed inside a tile, it pushes him out, adding to his speed (this is normally used at the beginning of a DDA to create an exciting, high-speed aspect). Consider the following map.

Example

In this map, every time the ninja collides with a tile it changes his velocity, either changing his direction, his speed, or both. The tiles have been placed to manipulate the movement of the ninja so that he avoids the rocket and the mines.

Example

In this map, the ninja is propelled into some tiles, which then propel him out, into the next tiles. Some of the tiles trap the ninja in a smaller are than he is supposed to be able to fit into, squeezing him, so he gains speed. The tiles also change his direction, allowing him to make turns in the map. The ninja was pushed through thin areas of tile, as to avoid risk of death, yet at greater speeds it's possible to go through larger areas of tiles, because the game is based on frames, and the ninja's velocity can be expressed mathematically in pixels per frame. If he's going fast enough, in one frame he can go from one side of a seemingly impassible tile to the other. This is called teleportation, and it will be discussed in more depth in the launch pad section.

Launch Pads

Launch pads are the most common methods of propulsion, probably because they are by far the easiest to use and manipulate. They instantly change the ninjas speed to a set value (based on the power of the pad, discussed later), meaning the ninja can around a large distance without much thought involved in the placement of the launch pad. They also instantly change the ninjas direction, so they can be used to make very close calls (also discussed later) for the ninja. Consider this map.

Example

The launch pads in this map are used to move the ninja out of danger at the last possible moment, and to push him into a safe area. A launch pad only needs to be placed exactly where the ninja is going to be, and it means that timing is not an issue, as with some other propulsions. A launch pad can also be used as a way to safely slow down a ninja who is travelling at a great speed in any direction, because (like I said before) a launch pad sets a ninjas velocity to a certain level, it doesnt simply add to it. That said, it is possible to change the speed that a launch pad will set for the ninja. A launch pads code looks like this.
2^396,468,0,-1
The first number (as with any object code) is the identification number of the launch pad. The second represents the launch pad's x-coordinate (this value begins at 0 for the very left of the screen and can go up to 768 for the very right). The third represents the pad's y-coordinate (this value begins at 0 for the top and goes up to 576 for the bottom). Every object has these three numbers at the beginning of their code. The following numbers represent the special attributes of whatever object it is. For a launch pad, there are two. The first (the 0) represents the x-component of the speed setting (how fast the ninja will move horizontally once he hits the pad). A negative value represents left, while a positive value represents right. Because the code in question has a value of 0 there, you know it will be a vertical launch pad. The second number (big surprise) represents the y-component of speed. A negative value represents up, while a positive represents down. So, we're looking at the code for a standard, upward-facing launch pad.
By changing the last two numbers, one can change the speed the launch pad will set the ninja to, in either direction. If the speed set is great enough, it is possible to teleport the ninja through objects, enemies or tiles. However, after this, there is generally something needed to slow down the ninja, otherwise he will die from high-speed collision or go flying out of the border and never return. It takes a while to get the schematics for teleporters just right, but the teleporter tutorial might help. Here's a map that shows a mastery of launch pad use.

Example

At the end, the ninja hits a code-edited launch pad, sending him through the tiles, where he collides with a standard launch pad, bringing him back to normal speed. Code-edited launch pads can provide an easy escape or method of transport for the ninja, wherever he is in the map. For this reason, using teleporters in a DDA is frowned upon, because it requires no skill. So unless you use it in some sort of innovative new idea, try to avoid using this technique in DDAs.

Doors

The Different Doors



A: Normal Door
B: Trap Door
C: Locked Door

Trap Door

Aside from the way trap doors can propel the ninja, a trap door (or locked or even normal) can be used in the same way as a tile, to change the ninjas velocity on collision, or offer him protection from most enemies.
Now, on to propulsion:
A trap door can be used to speed the ninja up in whatever direction he is going, or change his direction completely. To speed him up, a specific method is used, combining the switch and the door. A general rule of thumb for creating trap door propulsion to speed the ninja up is to place the key 3/4 of a space (using C-snap) from the door (see diagram). This increase in speed happens because the ninja hits the switch, thus locking the door, while some of his body is still behind where the door appears. When this happens, just like with tiles, the ninja is pushed out, and it adds to his speed. The more of the ninja behind the door when the switch is activated, the faster the ninja will be ejected.



Watch this map.

You will see that the switch is always activated when the ninja is still partially behind the door, pushing the ninja out.

Normal Door + Trap Door + Locked Door

This method of propulsion is more complicated. Firstly, to understand it, you must understand that if a trap door is created or a locked door is placed on top of a normal door, the set of doors acts as one normal door, allowing the ninja to pass, but deterring (most) enemies. However, if the ninja activates a key (either opening a locked door or creating a trap door) while he is partially in the door, he will be projected out. However, the door still acts like a normal door, and so he may pass through it again. This happens because when the ninja hits the switch, for the one frame that the door is unlocked (locked door) or created (trap door), the ninja interacts with it like a normal door, pushing him out. Jig has no idea what that means.

If the ninja passes through the correct combination of these doors (a normal door at the bottom, followed by alternating trap doors and locked doors) and there is another key that the he activates during the process (either opening a locked door or creating a trap door), he will bounce out of the door(s). The great thing about this glitchy propulsion is that it can be used multiple times. The effect is used in this map.

Example

Every time the ninja starts to pass through a door, he activates one switch, propelling him back upwards. Then he falls back and hits the door and again, activating a switch until there are no more switches, at which point he falls through the door. He falls through because when the switches are gone, the system acts as a single, normal door.

Normal Door + Gold Delay

This propulsion method is based on the fact that, if the right amount of gold is placed on the door (usually about 6 pieces) then the ninja will be partly inside the door when the last piece of gold is collected. For some unknown reason, the door shuts, pushing the ninja out, almost acting as a trap door having just been activated. The velocity with which the ninja is ejected at this time depends on how much of his body is still in the door when the last piece of gold is collected (again, very similar to trap door propulsion). Another cool thing that can be done with this idea is to rebound the ninja, pushing him backward (because he is actually in the other side of the door). Watch this map.

Example

This map has examples of the ninja being sped up by normal doors. It also shows the ninjas direction being altered, sometimes just a little and sometimes a lot (completely in the other direction even) by them. This method of propulsion also provides the ninja with protection, because normal doors deter enemies just like tiles and other doors.

Locked Door Glitch

(Everything in this section also applies to trap doors after they have already been created)

The locked door glitch, like teleporters, requires code editing. The code of a standard locked door looks like this:

9^708,588,0,0,29,21,1,0,0

To change it into a glitched door, change the third number after the ^ to NaN (Not a Number). A glitched door always appears vertical, even if it was initially placed at a horizontal position in Ned, because the number that you edited to make it glitched was the one that represented the doors orientation. When using them they may seem random but there are general rules that apply to them, as shown by this diagram.



A: This is what the door will always look like unless opened.
(N.B: A door will act like there is nothing there if it is opened)

B: This diagram comes as close as it is possible to representing how a glitched door acts. The red line is where the door appears to be, but in fact there is nothing there that interacts with the ninja. The one-ways represent the fact that the ninja can only travel through them one way, however, unlike normal one-way doors, they have corners that he can tilt off of (similarly to a tile or a standard locked door). The launch pads show which direction you will probably be launched in if you collide with that point, however the velocity at which they launch is seemingly random and depends on the speed and angle at which you collide with it. A lot of the time, the area will kill you, so careful testing is required to get the required launch.
Also, an interesting point in terms of protection is that the visible door offers no protection, while the one-ways act like one-way protection acting in the same direction as a normal one-way. See this example.



In this diagram, both doors are glitched. The upper rocket cannot detect the ninja because the upper door is protecting the ninja. The lower rocket can detect him and will fire, because the lower door only offers protection from it. For examples of all that has been explained above about glitched doors, watch this map.

Example

Thwumps

Thwumps are one of the most versatile objects that can be used in a DDA, and effective, and a popular method. In terms of propulsion, they can be used like tiles to change the ninjas velocity, they can be used to push the ninja, and they can be used to expel the ninja at high-speed by squeezing him between the thwump and a tile or another object. A major advantage to thwumps is that they can also be used multiple times, without creating another object or employing gold delay (discussed later) but they can be difficult to use because they move around, so they need to placed so that they move the ninja when wanted, and dont interact with the ninja when unwanted.
Firstly, the thwump moving the ninja like a tile is self-explanatory. If the ninja collides with a thwump, he changes velocity. If he finds himself inside a thwump for a frame, he is expelled out with force (similarly to with tiles or doors). Secondly, if the ninja hits the correct side of a retreating thwump, he is pushed along with the thwump. This can be used to push the ninja into objects, and then squeeze him out. To see examples of all uses of thwumps, watch this map.

Example

The ninja is pushed and squeezed by thwumps, giving him the required motion to finish the map.
Thwumps, when pushing the ninja can also squeeze him into another object or tile, giving him a large increase in velocity. It has actually been proven that thwumps can teleport the ninja through a tile by squeezing it. Marvel at this idea by wjbranson.

Example

Moving Walls

One very special usage of thwumps which many great DDA makers love to use is called the Moving Wall method. Its becoming more common, and was popularized by Lucidiums Pressed for Time. When placed in a specific area, half-way engulfed in a tile, a thwump is placed facing the inside of the wall. See this diagram.



In the diagram above, as long as there is any tile in the red area, the thwump will move in the direction of the arrow when touched. In the last two cases, a little more care is needed when placing the thwumps. They will not obey the special Moving Wall rules if they are placed using 1/2 snap. The third thwump in the diagram must be placed exactly one pixel above its 1/2 snap position to work. The last thwump must be placed exactly one pixel left of its 1/2 snap position to work. Both of these can be done by code-editing. Knowing that the 2 numbers directly after the 8^ represent the x- and y-coordinates of the object, you know how to manipulate its position to move it one pixel in the direction you want (youll be subtracting 1 to do this either way, from the x-coordinate for a leftward moving wall, or from the y-coordinate for an upward moving wall). This is a map with plenty of moving walls to demonstrate their impact in a DDA.


Example

False Tiles

Perhaps the most enigmatic of the many propulsion methods, false tiles are strange tiles that appear as 5 tiles with Q alignment (oddly, they take on the likeness of a 1 tile with Q alignment in thumbnails on Numa). However, they act nothing like this type of tile. Rather, when a ninja reacts with these tiles, he is either propelled out in a seemingly random direction and speed, or he passes directly through them, depending on the area of collision. Watch this map.

Example


As much as Id like to say I know all there is to know about this sort of thing, creating a DDA using false tiles is pretty much based on guess and check. Sure, some patterns can be seen when working with these strange things for long enough, but no definitive rules have been discovered yet. Perhaps in time. Because it is so seldom used in DDAs, this method is a good show of skill and poise, and after some experimentation, the style can also improve.

How to Make False Tiles

Making false tiles is fairly simple.
Step 1: Pick the space you want your tile to be.
By playtesting the map, youll know where the ninja will be in his path, and be able to decide where is the most effective space on which to place this tile.
Step 2: Place any tile you wish there.
I use E tiles.
Step 3: Return to the main menu and look at your level data.
Home, Page Down, ~. Also, I suggest highlighting the data to look at it, just to save you time, because the gray on white is not terribly easy on the eyes.
Step 4: Find the data for the tile you placed, and replace it with one of the false tile codes.
If you used an E tile, the code will appear as a 1. Refer to the next subdivision of the Manifesto for what codes create false tiles.

What is the Code for That, Anyway?
Despite the rarity of these false tiles, there are a multitude of different characters that represent them. Use any of the following to create a false tile.
Q R S T U V W X Y Z ~ ` ^ _ \ |
And thats just about all of them.

Here is a map that includes every type of propulsion mentioned.

Propulsion Grand Finale

Gold Delay

Placing gold on top of an object can permit the ninja to travel across the area where the object is without activating the object. This can be used to let the ninja pass through an object the maker wants him to activate later, it can allow the ninja to delay the time until he activates the object, or it can place the ninja partially inside an object (such as a thwump or bounce block), so as to be expelled from it, to increase his velocity when the gold runs out. Watch this map.

Example

It uses all three implementations of gold delay. Sometimes the ninja goes over a switch without activating it, sometimes he hits a launch pad and has a delayed launch, and sometimes he is ejected from inside an object when gold over the object depletes.

Enemies

Any great DDA maker will assure you that enemies are an integral part of a good DDA.
They are what make a DDA truly exciting and fun to watch. Rockets, laser drones, and sometimes guass turrets are effective enemies, while mines are stationary and boring, zap drones are easily manipulated, thwumps are more useful for propulsion than as an enemy, and chaingun drones often create problems (discussed later).

The Importance of Enemies

So what makes enemies so poignant in a given DDA? Well, simply put, if youre watching a little dude fly around and hes in no danger, theres no real point. So, enemies add an element of surprise, excitement, and flare to any DDA. A really good DDA will even have the watcher frightened for a time that the ninja might not actually survive. Watch this map.

Example

Well, earlier it was stated that over-usage of launch pads in a DDA was frowned upon. So what makes this DDA so successful? Well, watch it. Does it not excite you with its constant suspense? I find myself saying Woah how did he make it through that? Basically, enemies make this DDA good, and nothing else; take them away, and its crap. When adding enemies to your DDA, what you should look for is to make close calls.

Close Calls

Close calls are, as their name would suggest, a point in time in which the ninja is in extreme danger of being struck by a given enemy; a time in which the user may question the ninjas fortuity to escape the danger. What you saw in that last example DDA that made you so excited were close calls. Notice now that in basically every DDA already used in this post as an example, there are many good close calls.
Two things affect the quality of close calls: amount of close calls and closeness of calls. Unlike in most cases, the quantity is more important than the quality. If you have one very close call thats really exciting, but the rest of the DDA is boring bouncing around, the DDAs overall quality will be less than a DDA with a bunch of semi-close calls. Take this map as an example of a lot of close calls in a small space.

Example

While I personally defamed the map myself, one must admit its pretty fun to watch.

Adding Enemies

The biggest problem with enemies in a DDA and the reason many people shy away from using them to their full potential is that adding enemies can cause problems. Once youve added an enemy, and it works perfectly in the DDA, you can add another and have the whole thing go down the toilet. However, there is a trick to making it possible to add enemies without shooting yourself if the foot. The old method of this was to simply add all of your enemies at the beginning, and then shape the path of the DDA around them. This map is probably the best example of this.

Example

This is a good, fool-proof way of making a DDA, but if you want to go with the more add as you go approach, a simple method can be employed. When you want to add an enemy, do nothing after you add it. Then go to the main menu, and highlight the code of the last object you placed (at the end of the code). You should know how much of it is the code for the one object from the earlier discussions of code editing. Cut that and paste it at the beginning. With very few exceptions, this keeps all of the other enemies in working order and adds the new one.
This sounds odd, but the way objects (namely enemies) work in N is they go backward. Whichever object is at the end of the level data is the item that activates/is activated first (like with gold delay). A more thoughtful way of thinking of this principle is that the entire thing is in 3d. The last object placed is at the front, and everything placed before it is behind. Spacey�
Anyway, because of this principle of objects, the first object is activated last, so placing the code for an enemy at the very beginning does not affect the activation pattern of all the enemies placed earlier.

Cheating Death

Cheating death is a phenomenon that has lately had a lot of investigation by several innovative mapmakers. There are two types of cheating death.

Launch Pad Cheating Death

The more common type, launch pad cheating death is when an enemy (only certain ones work) strikes the ninja in the same frame as he hits a launch pad. In user levels or game mode, this causes him to become ghosted. He walks around and interacts only with tiles and doors, but cannot activate objects and does not open normal doors. However, in debug mode, he continues on as if nothing happened. This is a map that demonstrates a mastery of launch pad cheating death.

Example

Exit Door Cheating Death

Less explored is exit door cheating death, which can be done in many consecutive frames, because unlike a launch pad, the ninja can stand for a time inside the door. It works the same as launch pad cheating death. This map shows a great deal of uses of this method.

Example

Enemies that Work

In both cases, only certain enemies work. Rockets, thwumps, zap drones, mines and floor guards all work, while laser drones, guass turrets, and chaingun drones do not.

Variety

If you take only one thing away from this manifesto, then you really suck at gathering information, because jeez, this is a long-winded, all-inclusive manual to DDA making. However, this is the thing you should take away is Variety. Variety, variety, variety. Its the most important thing. Sure, there are some innovative maps (i.e. Lucidiums Gauge Invariance and Jigs Glitchtastic series) are great with just one type of propulsion, but in general, both in propulsion and in enemies, variety is the key. Make for many different types of close calls by having several different types of enemies. Use doors, pads, bounce blocks, thwumps, and everything else in your power to make your DDA as fresh and exciting as possible.

Happy mapping!

lord_day & Jig

#13234 1 2006-12-31T01:35:51Z Jiggerjaw
Tileset Madness Competition - Take 2.[/u]

For anyone who is fairly new or just had their head in a cave about a year ago, the original TCM was quite successful. Amlt requested today that I try to recreate that awesomeness. So, here we are. Everyone enjoy...

The Rules[/u]

This is a competition in which you create N art out of only tiles. As a map cannot be submitted to Numa without at least 1 object, you must add one object to your map, and I'd like it to be either hidden by the tiles that make up the level border, or far outside the border, such that it cannot be seen (preferably the former).
The maximum number of submissions by one user is 5.
That's pretty much it, although there is one other rule. If a map is deleted from Numa, it is automatically stricken from the competition. I will be checking Numa periodically to make sure this rule is conformed to.
If a submission is created using N-art Maker (and I should expect a sort of decency out of all of you regarding this sort of thing), it will not be accepted as a viable entry for the competition. I do this because, if it's not your work, then why should I accept it? It's like plagiarism.
Maps must have been submitted on or after today, December 30. So if you had a masterpiece in the past, too bad.

The Team[/u]

Amlt - Judge
Mr Mongoose - Judge
Jakel - Judge
Spiker - Back-up Judge
Ultima - Math-checker

The Judging[/b]

Each judge will rate each tileset out of 100 points.
Thumbnail of tileset: Out of 50 points
Full image of tileset: Out of 50 points
The sum of all 3 judges' total scores for each map will be tabulated to determine the final standings.

The Deadline

The deadline for the competition will be Thursday, February 1, at 12:00 AM GMT sharp. This will (most likely) not be extended for any reason. But if the competition is really nice and vibrant and I decide it needs a bit more time, I may. Also, if anyone comes up with a plausible reason for me to extend it, I'll consider it.

#474942 33 2008-10-08T00:50:38Z Jiggerjaw
I remember the last time I played N
It was summer.
We were on a road trip. My mother and father in the front seat, and I and my grandmother in the back
I had brought my laptop, just to pass the time, and I opened up N version 1.5. I started, as I did all those years ago, on level 00-0. And I worked my way through, never jumping from column to column; beating the game in the order it was intended to be beaten. I figured I had just enough battery life in the machine to complete the entire game. But, my skillswere not what they used to be
I struggled nownot like I used to when I played. I had trouble with levels I would've breezed through in my primeall the same, I was clearly an experienced player, doing all I could to impress my grandmother, who seemed a bit aloof throughout the whole ride. I didn't care. I was on a quest.
Totally oblivious to the outside world, I was nearing the end of the 50's column. "Brenden," my mother called back to me. I ignored, though I had heard her quite well. I passed 59-4, and finally said back to her, "What, mom? I'm on a quest."
"You're grandma's feeling very bad right now," she said, "and that's why the air conditioner is on full." I glanced at my grandmother, who seemed to gaze aimlessly, distantly, without any expression on her face. I was disheartened. I convinced myself she was merely tired, and continued on into the 60's column.
I was moving very quickly through the sixties, as they're one of the easiest sets of levels, but I had trouble, as usual, with 69-2. When I reached this problematic level, I heard my grandmother cough. It was terrible. Almost as if she was choking on something evil in her throat
The road trip had to be put on hold, as we turned for the hospital. But, I knew I only had just over an hour of battery left, and, through my worries, I played.
Still en route to the hospital, no more than ten minutes later, I had beaten the level. My sickly grandmother seemed to take a turn for the better, though it was still clear she was in need of medical care.
I frantically switched, over the next twenty minutes, between reassuring my grandma and reminding her to breathe, checking my battery life, and besting level after level. As crazy as it sounds, it seemed, with each new level, came a slight improvement in her condition. Perhaps it was just me, justifying my lust for entertainment over comforting her.
We arrived at the hospital, and I was starting episode 81. I took the laptop in with me, knowing I was going to beat the game in record time. I had a meager 45 minutes of battery life left, and was determined to get through the last two columns before the laptop died.
By the time we got our brave patient to a bed, I had reached the infamous Bricklayer, level 83-3. Her condition visibly worsened while I struggled with this frustrating level, but when it reached its resolution, she stopped her rapid decline. I was almost convinced at this moment that my playing had some inexplicable correlation with her physical state.
As I neared the 88th episode, and the hardest level of the game, my mother informed me that Grandma had been diagnosed with throat cancer. "Ridiculous," I said. But somehow, they had missed it, and now, it was too late.
But I played on.
I got fairly lucky with the latter portion of the 80's column, and went through it with still twenty minutes to spare on my battery. I played, now, frantically, knowing somewhere within me that, if I didn't beat the gameif my laptop diedso did she.
I couldn't let that happen.
The doctor approached my mother with a concerned look on his face as I was waiting for level 98-3 to load. "Do you want us to pull the plug," he asked, "or keep her alive for as long as she's got? It's never an easy decision." I couldn't let them take her off life support. I had to try. I tugged at my mother's sleeve, and implored her to make the right choice. She humored me.
With only two minutes of battery life left, I reached the final episode. Tears in my eyes, I played, though shaking like three diabetics, with more grace than I had ever exhibited playing any game in my life. I drifted effortlessly through the first four levels, leaving 45 seconds for the final level.
I died. And I died again. And I died again. I watched the last three seconds drip out of my laptop's battery, the last three breaths, out of my dying grandmother's chest.
She died, TS.
I haven't played N since.
